=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.98)
cation, en

ROM STATE DEPARTMENT FOR OPNAV:
Following received 2:29 p.m., April 18, 1941 from
Tamp1co
dated 9 a.m., April 18
It is stated in today's EL MUNDO that of the six Germans
of the crew of the ORINOCO who escaped two were hidden away on
the NAKO MARU at Tampico. It is added that the others were
taken on board by the NAKO MARU while at sea. The stowing away
is probable, but the story of the pick-up at sea is improbable,
as the Japanese boat departed three days in advance of the
departure of the sailboat which it is said the Germans commandeered
for escape. The names of the Germans are: Henert, Doesel, Koukel,
Holdorf, Meyer, and Seinkerk.
Collins

Tampioo, regarding continued heavy movements of strategio

telegram, it 1s not clear whether the 3,800 tons of lead

85

57

of this Department that nothing has happened. Nevertheless,
I wonder whether you might find it desirable and practicable
to give some pushes in the right quarters.

The Department ha
outPed Trom the Treasury Depart-
ment a copy of a confidential official report by the Deputy
Colleotor at Port San Luis, California, which states that
from April 28 to May 24, inclusive, eight Japanese tankers
are scheduled to arrive in California.
According to the Deputy Collector, each vessel will
varry approximately 85,000 barrels (a total of 680,000 bar-
rels or 28,900,000 gallons) of a special grade of crude
oil known as "Special San Joaquin Valley Crude".
The Deputy Collector states that he was informed by
local representatives of the Standard Oil Company, the
PS/LB
shipping company, that this grade of crude oil has a very
high ootane and is used in Japan for the distillation of
aviation motor fuel and that it is of a much higher octane
than that of the crude oils usually shipped to Japan.

2.

addressed to the Secretary of State, it was requested that

dates of arrival and departure, last ports of call, and
ports for which bound of the Japanese vessels specified.
chused
3.

to the Consulate General at Sydney clarify the desire for.

bound regardless of whether they may be U.S. or foreign

It would appear from a careful examina-
tien of this correspondence that the request
from the Navy for routing messages in regard
to the movements of Japanese naval vessels
and the request of the Military Intelligence
at Manila for information on this subject were
entiroly unrelated, and that the Military
Intolligonce at Manila acted upon its own
initiative. From MID's memorandum of March 12
it would not appear that the War Department
could be disoomfited at any time in the future
by not getting this information. It  under-
stood that Military Intelligence at Man lejhas
made other unapproved and somewhat unusuaf
requests of the Consul at Nanila recently

by the attaohed telegram.

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.94)

-20-41
We are informed by the Ministry of
Economic Warfare that arrangements have been made
to refer to London all requests by Japanese ships
for bunkers and other facilities at British ports
and at other ports where British control can be
exercised over bunker supplies.
Deliveries of bunkers are being res-
tricted to quantities sufficient only to carry the
ship to the next port of call, and the N.E.I.
authorities have agreed to restrict deliveries
similarly. These measures havebben introduded
in order to have machinery available to delay or
to hold Japanese ships at our ports and also to
reduce the risk that these ships may supply fuel
to enemy raiders.

The High Commissioner reports in his telegram of

practically all of the oll and water supplies to ships
for monthly reports of amounts supplied to Japanese

Government thinks desirable. It is believed that com-

they have not been canvassed since the amount of bunker
coal sold in the Philippines is negligible

Prior to the Fall of 1939 the "K" Line service was largely maintained by
wessels of the Norway and Venice Maru type with a sailing approximately every five weeks.
Prior to the Fall of 1939 the N,Y.K. Line service was largely maintained
by the 12 knot ahips the Ginyo Maru, Rakuyo Maru, Takoaka Maru, Boluyo Maru (burned), and
the 16 knot ship, the Heiyo Maru, on approximately a sailing per month.
From the above figures it may be seen that the two services have greatly
meressed and improved their sailings from the viewpoint of both tonnage and speed. The
infommation has been gathered from Lloyd's Register of Shipping and it is believed that ts
ressels constructed since 1937 are capable of greater speed than indicated.
speed of these two vessels has been estimated from their horsepower and size.
April 29, 1941.

The Secretary of State,

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's 21
PS
circular telegraphic instruction dated December 17, 1940,
9 p.m., concerning the desire of the Navy Department to
obtain certain information regarding the movements of
Japanose vessels. Reference is also made to the
Department'

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
Department's telegram dated March 18, 1941, 5 p.m., to
the Consulate General at Sydney, in further regard to
this matter.
It is presumed that the Department sought the
assistance of the consular officers in obtaining this
information by reason of the non-presence in Australia
at that time of a naval attaché. Since his arrival in
Australia, the Naval Attaché has made arrangements with
the Australian Department of the Navy to receive such
information regularly, and Commander Causey has already
telegraphed one such item of information direct to the
Navy Department. After consultation with the Supervising
Consulate General, the Consulate at Melbourne, and
Commander Causey, it has appeared that no further effort
should be made by consular officers to obtain and forward
such information, and in the absence of instructions from
the Department to the contrary, the system at present in
effect will obtain, namely, that Commander Causey secure
and transmit such information direct to the Navy Department
in Navy code.
Respectfully yours,
Ghumu
John R. Minter

800.8591
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
4√1
I have the honor to refer to the Legation's
despatch no. 119, dated Apri1 10, 1941, with regard to
the method of transmitting of information on the move-
monts of Japanese vessels. The Naval Attaché has now
recei

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
received a telegram from the Navy Department whioh reads
Tollowa:
"Have requested the State Department
instruct Consuls Australia New Zealand
report foreign merchant ship movements
less British. Suggest you cooperate."
It is noted from this telegram (a) that the
Navy Department desires information on the movements
of all foreign merchant ships except British (Earlier
requests were for movements of Japanese vessels only.),
(b) that it apparently still desires American consuls
to seek to obtain this information, and (c) that it wishes
the Naval Attaché to cooperate.
The inference is drawn from the foregoing that no
matter which officer obtains the information it is to be
telegraphed to the Department of State, and not to the
Navy Department, and by the consular officer at the par-
ticular port, and not by the Naval Attaché. I wish to
point out that the consular officers are not in a position
to obtain this information. They have no standing with
the Australian Nawal Board, and actually efforts by con-
sular officers to obtain certain types of secret informa-
tion are resented in some quarters. Both through the
efforts of the Legation and through the personal efforts
of Commander Causey, the Australian Naval Board, the only
authority in Australia which could authorize the release
of such information, had agreed to supply the Naval Attaché
with periodic reports. It seemed logical that as the
Department of State's request for information had been
at the instanoe of the Navy Department, the establishment
of

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
of an office of a naval attaché would be the signal for
instituting a practice of direct reports by the Naval
Attaché to the Navy Department.
The foregoing conclusion does not arise, I am sure,
from a disposition on the part of any Foreign Service
officer stationed in Australia to shirk a responsibility,
but has been drawn because of its logical connection with
efficiency.
It would be greatly appreciated if the Department
would provide the Legation with an instruction which would
clear up the uncertainty as to who should collect and
transmit this information.
Respectfully yours,
Johndmn
John R. Minter
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim
121/885.91/812.2
JRM/meg
To the Department in triplicate.
Copy to Sydney Consulate General.
Copy to Melbourne Consulate.
Copy to Commander Causey at Melbourne.

THE UNITED STATES HIGH COMMISSIONER

Memorendum

The Piles
Byr
Mr. Bell, Legal Adviser
Subjects
Radiogram No. 193, Mey 10, from the Seerotary of
State, and controi of supplies to Japanese ships.
On receipt of this radiogram, May l5th, I immediately
oonferred with Pete Grimm, of the Luzon Stevedoring Company,
and also with Howard Smith, the Quarantine Ofricer, because
of their close knowledge of what goes on on the waterfront.
Both were most helpful and will cooperate in every way
possible. Through them I confirmed my undorstanding that
substantially all bunker, diesel and lubricating oil are
furnished in the Philippines by four large companies: The
Standard Vaouum; Caltex; Tidewater Associated; and The
Asiatio Petroleum. I ascertained that the supply of water
to vessels is largely under the oontrol of the Luson and
Bailey Steredoring Companies, whioh can be counted on to
cooperate fully in the event that it should be necessary
to ourtail supplies to Japanese ships. There seems to be
one Japanese company whioh supplies some water to vessels,
but it can be controlled when necessary through the Metro-
politem Water Company from whieh it obtains water.
... On May 16th

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.96)
On May lsth I cenferred seriatim with Mr. Roeks, Presi-
Sont of the Standard Vacuum, Mr. Roesholm of Caltex, Mr.
Herfmemn of Tidewater Associated, and Mr. Cramford of Aslasie
Petreloum. They were mere than cooperative and, as a matter
of fast, already have had their eye on the situation. No
abmormal supplies of fuel, diesel or lubricating oil are
knom to have been supplied to any Japanese vessels either
direetly or through the motor fishing vessels in harbors.
Apparemtly such fishing vessels have been using only their
normal supply. These gentlemen were about as suspicious of
Freneh vessels as of Japanese vessels and recently a Preneh
vesael desired to fill not only her fuel oil tanks but also
her deep tanks. She feiled to seoure the oil desired for
this purpose.
These gentlemen will supply this ofrice monthly, com-
moneing with April l94l, with the amounts of oil furnished
to Japanese vessels and also the amounts furnished to French
vessels. They will also immediately bring to the attention
of this office any orders for Japanese or French vessels of
abmormal quantities and any suspoious ciroumstanes which they
may observe. Pete Grimm and Howard Smith will also immediately
netify this ofrise of any suspicious ciroumstances which they
mmy observo.
These oil companies said they would gladly cooperate in
outting down supplies to Japanese or other vessels, should

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.97)
is be desired, to quantities smfricient only to sake the
vessels to their next port. They are satisfied that volumtary
sooperetien can also so control supplies of water and also of

damand for buaker coal here. Probably most of the coal is

I thought it better at this time not to take the matter up

as it seems best to disolose the State Department's request

As an additional precaution, I conferred also on May
16th with Commander Portz, the Navy Neutrality Officer, and
also with Commander Hill, the Navy Intelligence Officer. I
diselosed to both of them the radiogram of the State Depart-

ordering unusual supplies or any suspicious ciroumstances

those to whom it may supply fuel oil here from its Londen

insluding shortage of bottoms, with the result that the oil
eompemies are interested primarily in looking after their
regular customers and are not much interested in supplying
oil to sporadic customers. r. Rooke said that he under-

north of Hong Kong with the result that oil supplies in
Shanghai and that neighborhood have been greatly curtailed

where oil is required, and various other uses of oil have
been greatly restricted.
Madrigal & Company has an oil tanker whioh is now en

of oil for the Standard Vacuum Oil Company. Fernandez
Hermanos, Ino., has an oil tanker which, however, is used
to supply the needs of his own concerns. It may be that De
La Rama & Co. sometimes brings in some deep tanks of fuel
oil and it may be that a few other ships do the same. These
amounts are not very material, will be known and can be con-
trolled. The real comtrol is in the four companies above
mensioned.

vessel, arrived at Manzanillo from Los Angeles. The

Enclosure (a) shows pictures of Japanese steamers
that have recently mede calls at West Coast ports of
South America. These pictures were not all taken et
Callao, as it is very difricult to persuade a photogrepher

894.8591/530
reason.

as the two pictures do not appear to be of the same ship.
An iuformant states thet the average speed of te
slow Japanese steamers 1s 13-14 knots. The average speed
of the faster steamers is 17-18 knots, but they are capable
of making 20 knots. He furtier reports that the Jepanese
steamers are generally listed in Lloyds Register at a slower
speed than they can actually make.
CoMENT: Infomation is requested as to whether or not
the Department has full information at hand on the Japanese
merchant ships, including pictures. It is reguested that
this office he adyised by despetch if further pictures are
desired.

8
At 7 a.m. this morning the KIYOKAWA MARU arrived here
from Los Angeles with 561 tons of artificial silk and Japanese
general cargo for Manzanillo. The vessel also disembarked one
Japanese passenger for Mexico City. Amount of general cargo in

believed that the vessel wil1 load 468 flasks mercury, 1500 tons

My dear Mr. Secretary;
UEPARTMENT OF STATE
194
Receipt is an mowledged of your letter of June 6, 194l, with-
out file number, nclosing a copy of an aide-memoire from the
Japanese Embassy dated June 3, 194l, relative to the search of the
894.8591/554
Japanese S. S. NISSHIN MARU at San Francisco on May 29, 1941, by
customs officials and the seizure therefrom of certain documents
which were redelivered to the vessel later that day.
The Department's records show that the NISSHIN MARU arrived
at San Francisco on May 27, 194l; that she was boarded by customs
officers at 4:45 p.m. on that date, and a customs guard remained
aboard the vessel all night; and that on the following morning the
customs searching squad boarded the vessel for the routine customs
search for narcotics, other contraband, and for mail matter carried
694.8591
contrary to law.
The records further show that the customs searching squad
seized one 5-gram bottle of heroin (diacetyl morphine) which was
found in the narcotic medicinal stores, and the importation of
which is unconditionally prohibited; that they found a bag contain-
ing pamphlets, documents, and envelopes of miscellaneous papers,
all in the Japanese language, which they considered might be ir-
regularly carried mail matter or might contain information pertain-
ing to narcotics or other contraband; that they took possession of
JUL.8三1941
FILED
the bag and its contents and, after giving the master a receipt
therefor, conveyed it to the Customs Assembly Room for examination
in the usual manner, and that the officers reached the Customs
Assembly Room about 4:00 p.m., for further inspection of the con-
tents of the bag and instructions from the Collector.
At 4:30 p.m. that date, the records show, following a protest
lodged with the Assistant Collector of Customs by a representative
PS/DE
of the Japanese Consulate General that the contents of the bag
consisted of codes and other ship's documents, instructions were
issued by the Assistant Collector that the bag and contents be im-
mediately returned to the NISSHIN MARU, and these instructions were

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
complied with. On the return trip to Port Costa, which is ap-
proximately 35 miles from the Customs Assembly Room, over a
road a portion of which is in bad condition, the officers were
delayed by tire trouble. A further delay was experienced due
to the reluctance of the chief officer, in the absence of the
master, to furnish a receipt for the returned material. The
receipt for the documents was obtained at about 8:30 p.m., May
28, 1941, at which time an assent to forfeiture of the 5-gram
bottle of heroin was also obtained.
Very truly yours,
Maaa
HERBERT E. GASTON,
Assistant Secretary of the Treasury.
4
The Honorable,
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.

FROM STATE DEPARTMENT FOR OPNAV:
Following received 7-5-41, 2:04 p.m.
from
Rio de Janeiro. dated 7-5-41, 3 p.m.
8
I have been told by the American Vice Consul at
Santos that since the seven Japanese vessels, which
were destined for South American ports, have been called
to Japan, Isk agents have been given directions to speed
up the loading of these ships. Scheduled calls at the
ports of Buenos Aires and Santos are being omitted by two
of these vessels.
CAFFERY

Department of State
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Berle:
As of possible interest to you, I wish to advise that
information has been received from a reliable confidential source
that agents of the Osaka-Shosen-Kaisha Line recently received
instructions thet no more ships from South American ports will sail
for Japan via South Africa, but are to proceed via Panama.
